Foreword by the Governor of the National Bank of Moldova
(NBM)
  
 
  
  
 
 
The activities carried out by the NBM in 2017 were in line with the reforming
strategy embraced by the Bank in 2016, among the main goals of which can
be listed the stabilisation of monetary conditions, the strengthening of the
banking sector and the improvement of the national legislative and regulatory
framework. These objectives aim at preventing the recurrence of law violations
that resulted in a bank fraud during 2012 -2014 and triggered the ﬁnancial
crisis that followed. A catalyst to the implementation of these reforms is the
Agreement signed by the Republic of Moldova with the International Monetary
Fund (IMF) on 7 November 2016.
In 2017, the NBM, in cooperation with the country’s authorities, successfully
fulﬁlled its commitments made to the IMF, the latter having declared as successful the ﬁrst and
the second reviews of the progresses achieved in programme implementation, the fact that was
conﬁrmed in the IMF decisions of 1 May and 20 December 2017.
Developments that took place on the international market produced mixed effects on the Moldovan
economy and, consequently, on the monetary policy of the NBM. On the one hand, a positive
evolution of global economy (+3.7% in 2017) created favourable conditions for economic growth
in the Republic of Moldova. In the European Union – the main trading partner of our country – the
economic growth reached 2.5% and the historically high unemployment rate has decreased. In
the East, after two consecutive years of recession, the economy of the Russian Federation grew
by 1.5% in 2017. On the other hand, the increase in global demand was the main cause of the
rise in international commodity prices, including food and energy. A faster rise in oil prices was
partially driven by the cut in extraction quota approved under the agreement of the Organisation
of Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC).
Against this background, the Republic of Moldova recorded a robust economic growth as well as a
temporary increase in domestic consumer prices.
• Thus, in 2017, the gross domestic product (GDP) grew by 4.5%, being driven by the increase
in domestic consumption and agricultural production output. The economic growth, recorded
in the main countries of emigration of Moldovan citizens, increased the volume of remittances,
which in turn contributed to the increase in the purchasing power of local consumers. In the
second half of the year, a revival of industrial activity and a strong growth of exports were
also recorded.
• In 2017, the average annual growth rate of consumer price index (CPI) recorded 6.6%, 0.2
p.p. higher than in the previous year. Annual inﬂation of consumer prices rose from 3% in
January to 7.3% in December 2017, being driven by the rises in energy prices, food prices
and tariffs for regulated services. However, annual core inﬂation continued to ﬂuctuate within
the inﬂation target range, recording an average level of 4.8%. At the time of drafting this
report, the annual consumer price inﬂation fell to 3.7%, according to April 2018 statistics.
In 2017, as forecasts suggested a steady decline of the inﬂation rate, the NBM maintained its
policy of lowering the base interest rate, yet at a slower pace than in 2016: from 9.0% in early
2017 to 6.5% at the end of the year. At the same time, in order to absorb the liquidity surplus
and improve the transmission mechanism of monetary policy, the NBM increased the minimum
level of required reserves held in Moldovan lei from 35% to 40%. The minimum level of required
reserves held in freely convertible currencies (FCC) was maintained at the same level of 14%. The
NBM’s monetary policy, the decline of inﬂation projections as well as the favourable conditions
existing on the money market led to a signiﬁcant improvement in the level of borrowing by the
8


Ministry of Finance on the domestic capital market. Thus, in 2017, the average interest rate on
government securities (GS) recorded 6.7% compared to 15.8% in 2016.
The liquidity surplus, recorded in the system, was largely the result of the increased inﬂow of
foreign currency remittances to Moldovan individuals, totalling 1.2 billion U.S. dollars, which
represents a growth of 11.2%, compared to the previous year. As a result, during 2017, the
Moldovan lei strengthened by 14.4% against the US dollar and by 2.3% against the euro. The
appreciation of the Moldovan lei against the U.S. dollar was partially driven by the weakening of
the U.S. currency against the euro, which fell from 1.0454 at the beginning of the year to 1.1935
against the euro in December 2017. Therefore, in order to prevent the excessive volatility and
appreciation of the Moldovan lei, the NBM, throughout 2017, intervened on the forex market by
purchasing the equivalent of 434 million U.S. dollars. As a result, the ofﬁcial foreign currency
reserves reached a record level of approximately 2.8 billion U.S. dollars by the end of 2017, thus
amply ensuring the equivalent of 5.5 months of imports of goods and services.
Along with the measures aiming to stabilise monetary conditions, the NBM has taken decisive
actions for strengthening the banking sector. To this end, the NBM conducted targeted inspections
to check the quality and transparency of the banks’ shareholders, veriﬁed related party
transactions, as well as performed asset quality assessments. Full-scope on-site inspections have
been conducted and completed at seven out of the eleven licensed banks. Those inspections
uncovered and eliminated the main uncertainty sources affecting the banking sector, whereas the
applied remedial measures, including those targeting capital retention, have signiﬁcantly
increased the sector’s resilience. Thus, during the reporting period, capitalisation and liquidity of
the banking sector reached 31% and 55%, respectively.
The implementation of prudential
supervision measures helped to restore the stability, transparency and credibility of the banking
sector, both on the internal and the external market.
For the ﬁrst time in the last ten years, a reputable foreign investor entered the Moldovan banking
market. In November 2016, Banca Transilvania (the second largest commercial bank by assets
in Romania) together with the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD)
announced its intention to acquire 39.2% of shares in BC "VICTORIABANK" S.A. The deal was
closed in January 2018, thus clearing the way for the NBM to end the intensive supervision of one
of the three systemic banks, which operate under the special supervision regime since 2015. In
cooperation with international experts, the NBM developed a strategy for the sale of the blocked
shares of the other two banks, to ensure their stability and good governance. The Parliament of the
Republic of Moldova backed the strategy of attracting foreign investors by amending the Law on
the Administration and Privatisation of Public Property, the updated version of which entered into
force on January 1, 2018. The legislative amendments provide the possibility of State interference,
under prescribed conditions, in the purchase and sale of the shares issued by systemic banks to
reputable investors. At the time of drafting this report, an international consortium ﬁled a formal
request with the NBM to acquire a stake of 41.09% in BC "MOLDOVA - AGROINDBANK" S.A.
On January 1, 2018, a new Law on Banking Activity, which was adopted on October 6, 2017, was
ﬁnally enacted. It has a major role for the Moldovan banking system as it aligns the national
legislation with the EU Directives and introduces the best international practices (also known
as "Basel III" standards) in the ﬁeld of prudential supervision. The law has been drafted during
2015-2017 in cooperation with experts from the central banks of Romania and the Netherlands
under the European Twinning programme. In order to allow licensed banks to adapt gradually
to the new rules, the law provides for a 2-year transition period. During this time, the NBM will
issue about 30 regulatory acts to align the local regulatory framework with the new banking law.
The impact study, which was carried out by the NBM based on ﬁnancial statements as of 30 June
2017, conﬁrms that all banks comply with the new prudential requirements. In order to prevent a
possible erosion of own funds before the implementation of the new rules, the NBM recommended
to a number of banks to refrain from paying out proﬁts for 2017.
In 2017, the aggregate proﬁt of the banking sector recorded approximately 1.5 billion Moldovan
lei, having increased by 8.6%, compared to 2016. The balance of bank loans, expressed in MDL,
continued to decrease, though at a slower rate, having dropped to 33.5 billion lei in December
2017, thus recording a decrease in value of 3.7%, year-on-year. This apparent decrease can be
partly explained by the effect of the exchange rate difference, as approximately 40% of loans
were extended in foreign currencies, which have depreciated against the Moldovan lei. At the
9


same time, the annual volume of the newly extended loans increased by 8.9%, compared to 2016,
amounting to 24.5 billion lei. The annual growth rate of the new loans extended in domestic
currency reached approximately 20%. The positive dynamics of lending was driven by lower
average interest rates and increased demand from individuals. The evolution of the lending
activity will continue to depend on the revival of demand generated by the corporate sector. The
balance of non-performing loans recorded 18.4%. Based on the recommendation of the NBM, the
licensed banks created additional reserves and ensured an 80% coverage rate of non-performing
loans in December 2017. Banks continued to take deposits, the balance of which reached 59.9
billion lei at the end of 2017, thus having increased by 9%, year-on-year. Corporate deposits
recorded the fastest growth rate, having increased by over 18% in one year. The deposit structure,
analysed by currencies, denotes a steadily increasing conﬁdence placed by local population and
economic agents in the domestic currency: as of December 2017, 57.2% of deposits were placed
in MDL, compared to 53.5% as of December 2016 and 42.4% as of September 2015, in the midst
of the ﬁnancial crisis.
The increased conﬁdence of the population in the Moldovan lei and the stability of the exchange
rate allowed the NBM to launch, in June 2017, the project to introduce in circulation metal coins
with denomination of 1 lei and 2 lei. After an in-depth analysis of cash circulation in the Republic
of Moldova and of existing international practices, the project was extended to include coins
with denomination of 5 lei and 10 lei. Under this project, the NBM organised public consultations
with consumers, economic agents, local and central authorities. New metal coins were put into
circulation on February 28, 2018. The introduction of metal coins will allow the NBM to generate
savings in production costs, which are estimated at approximately 300 million lei in 20 years.
Without prejudice to its fundamental objective and its core competencies, the NBM pursues to
optimise spending. The increase of the minimum level of required reserves held in Moldovan lei
from 35% to 40% had as its corollary objective the reduction of the monetary policy expenditures.
At the same time, a signiﬁcant increase in the volume of certiﬁcates issued by the NBM had a
reverse effect. Revenues gained from foreign currency transactions recorded a decrease, being
driven by the appreciation of the Moldovan lei. As a result, in 2017, the NBM recorded a loss of
95.31 million lei, which decreased by 64%, compared to 2016. At the end of 2017, the NBM’s
statutory capital of 2.35 billion lei accounted for approximately 5.1% of total monetary bonds.
The year 2017 was the ﬁrst fully operational year of the NBM’s Supervisory Board. It consists of
seven members, of which four members are independent advisors who do not hold executive
positions at the NBM. The Supervisory Board had an intensive year having held 10 meetings during
the year. Three independent members of the Supervisory Board form the Audit Committee, which
monitors the ﬁnancial reporting process, the efﬁciency of the internal control system, the activity
of the Internal Audit Department, as well as performs other duties that ensure the NBM’s effective
supervision of the banks. During 2017, the Executive Board has held 77 meetings at which 369
decisions were examined and approved.
In 2017, in order to improve the decision-making process, to streamline business processes and to
eliminate potential conﬂicts of interest, the NBM reorganised a number of departments according
to the best international practices. Simultaneously, the NBM reduced the number of managerial
positions by 4%, compared to 2016, and launched a strategy aiming at reducing the number of
support positions as opposed to core activity functions. In cooperation with a foreign consultancy
company, the industry leader on international market, the NBM has launched a reform of the
human resources management system. The bank’s internal reform and reorganisation were meant
to strengthen the institutional and human capacities of the NBM in order to provide a proactive
and high-quality public service.
We are determined to continue strengthening the banking sector and promoting its increased
transparency, as well as to implement reforms in other areas, such as monetary policy, ﬁnancial
stability, payment systems, improving the quality of the domestic currency among others.
These goals were included in the NBM’s Strategic Plan for 2018-2020. The new strategy aims
at consolidating the achievements of 2017 and focusing efforts in areas that require further
improvement. A key objective of the new strategy is to enhance external communication and
ﬁnancial education of population through media, web page publications, and social networking.
10


Looking at the tasks ahead, there is still plenty to do. However, 2017 has certainly represented
a year of signiﬁcant breakthrough in the consolidation of the banking sector, which today is
increasingly regarded by population as more safe and reliable.
Sergiu Cioclea
11


Chapter 1
External environment
1.1
Global
economy,
ﬁnancial
and
commodity markets
Global economic activity continued to improve
in 2017. According to IMF estimates, global
economic activity accelerated by 3.7%, which
represented a 0.5 p.p. improvement compared
to 2016. Economic growth was broad-based,
with signiﬁcant economic recovery registered
in Europe and Asia. Advanced economies grew
by an average of 2.3%, compared to 1.7%
recorded
in
2016.
The
acceleration
of
economic growth in developed economies has
been fuelled by stimulative measures, such as
"quantitative easing policies" promoted in
recent years by many countries.
Among
advanced economies, the highest growth rate
was recorded in the euro area, 2.5%. Growth in
the euro area was fuelled by higher internal
consumption and increased conﬁdence among
consumers, producers and investors in the
ability
of
the
European
community
to
overcome challenges and carry out structural
reforms. Within the euro area, economy has
grown by 2.2% in Germany, by 1.8% in France,
by 3.1% in Spain and by 1.5% in Italy. The U.S.
economy has grown by 2.3%, compared to
1.5% in 2016.
A slower growth rate was
recorded
in
the
United
Kingdom
(1.7%)
following diminishing capital in the economy
on the background of uncertainties generated
by Brexit (Chart 1.1).
Emerging and developing economies grew, on
average, by 4.7% in 2017. This growth rate
represented a 0.3 p.p. increase compared to
2016. China’s economy grew by 6.8% in 2017.
After
two
consecutive
years
of
economic
recession, the Russian Federation’s economy
grew by 1.5%. Higher oil prices and absorption
of economic shocks generated by sanctions
imposed on Russia by the EU and the U.S.
have
helped
it
overcome
an
economic
downturn.
Against the background of an
improving economy of the Russian Federation,
the economy of the CIS countries grew, on
average, by 2.2% in 2017 (Chart 1.1).

Overall, in 2017, the U.S. dollar depreciated
against other international currencies.
In
December 2017, the U.S. dollar index (USDX)1
fell to its three-year low of 91.8%. The impact
of the presidential election in November 2016
was followed by the difﬁculties encountered
with the U.S. Congress’s adoption of the tax
reform and the protectionist position of the
President Donald Trump. Only in October 2017,
after a consensus was reached to adopt tax
reforms, the U.S. dollar appreciated gradually
against other international currencies.
The
Federal
Reserve
System
(the
Fed)
continued
tightening
its
monetary
policy,
having increased interest rates three times
during 2017.
At the same time, the Fed’s
measures
did
not
contribute
to
the
appreciation of the U.S. dollar as its decisions
to raise interest rates were anticipated by the
ﬁnancial markets. The Fed’s tightening of the
monetary
policy
still
contrasts
with
ultra-stimulating monetary policies promoted
by other advanced economies (Chart 1.1).
In 2017, the diverging monetary policies of the
Fed and the ECB caused the European single
currency to appreciate, on average, by 2.0%
against the U.S. dollar.
The uncertainties
surrounding
Brexit
negotiations
have

on
average, by 0.2% and 2.9%, respectively,
while the Ukrainian hryvnia and the Turkish lira
depreciated
against
the
U.S.
dollar,
on
average, by 4.1% and 20.8%, respectively. The
negative evolution of the Ukrainian hryvnia
reﬂected negative dynamics maintained in the
economy, despite a large inﬂow of foreign
ﬁnancial aid. The depreciation of the Turkish
lira reﬂects, to a large extent, the increase of
the
current
account
deﬁcit
and
the
consequences of the stimulative monetary
policies promoted in the previous years (Table
1.1).
The year 2017 was marked by an increase in
international
commodity
prices
after
two
consecutive years of decline. The main factors
that caused the increase were the depreciation
of
the
U.S.
dollar
and
the
increase
in
international oil prices, which are used as a
benchmark for other price indices, as well as
growth in global demand. Stimulative policies
promoted
during
the
last
quarters
also
contributed to these developments. Thus, in
2017, the average price of Urals oil reached
53.1 U.S. dollars/barrel, this represented a
26.9% increase compared to 2016.
The
increase in the international prices for oil also
determined a slower decline in import prices
for natural gas supplied from the Russian
Federation. In 2017, the average import price
of natural gas from the Russian Federation was
165.5
U.S.
dollars/1000
m3,
thus
having
decreased
by
14.5% compared to
20162.
International prices on the main food groups
have risen, on average, by 8.1% in 2017
(Chart 1.3).

signed by the OPEC countries on 30 November
2016, as well as the agreement signed on 10
December 2016 with 11 non-OPEC countries,
to reduce oil production output in the ﬁrst half
of 2017. Subsequently, at the OPEC meeting
held on 30 November 2017, the agreement
was extended through the end of 2018. Thus,
the OPEC oil supply in 2017 remained almost
unchanged
(+0.1%),
compared
to
2016,
having recorded 39.3 mbd.

supply in 2017 represented 98.0 mbd, up 0.8%
compared to 2016.

the
international
oil
market
recorded
an
average deﬁcit of 0.5 mbd (Chart 1.4).

1.2
Evolution of important
economies
In 2017, the U.S. gross domestic product grew
by 2.3%, a 0.8 p.p. improvement compared
to 1.5% in 2016. The acceleration of the U.S.
economic growth was reﬂected in the data on
wage growth, acceleration of inﬂation growth
rate and low level of unemployment. In 2017,
unemployment rate gradually declined from
4.8% in January 2017 to 4.1% in December
2017. Against this background of increased oil
prices and domestic consumption, consumer
prices in the U.S. recorded a slight increase with
an annual average of 2.1%. These outcomes
determined the Fed to increase interest rate
three times in 2017.
In the reporting year, euro area economy grew
by 2.5%, having recorded its highest economic
growth since the 2008-2009 global ﬁnancial
crisis. Industrial production grew on average,
by 3.0% in euro area and by 3.3% in the
European Union. Strong exchange rate of the
European single currency contributed to the
increase in imports and exports by 9.7%, and
7.1%,
respectively.
The
decline
of
unemployment rate to 8.6%,
recorded in
December 2017, is another indicator of the
revival
of
European
economies
and
the
effectiveness
of
structural
reforms
implemented
during
the
previous
years.
Optimistic macroeconomic data determined
the ECB to reduce its asset purchases from 60
to 30 billion euro, starting January 2018 (Table
1.2).
Although monetary policy measures
undertaken by the ECB over the last few years
have
greatly
contributed
to
improving
inﬂationary outlook, reaching its 2.0% inﬂation
target in the medium term still remains a
challenge for the ECB.

After the UK decided to exit the EU, economic
growth continued to slow down throughout
2017. GDP grew by 1.7%, having recorded a
slower growth rate compared to 2016. Slower
growth caused by a fall in real wages affected
private
consumption
growth
rate
and
uncertainties associated with Brexit leading to
a decrease in investments. A negative trend in
labour productivity remains a concern for the
UK economy, being partly attributed to the
increase in the number of low paid jobs.
In
2017, for the ﬁrst time after the Brexit vote,
unemployment rate fell to 4.3%, partly driven
by the reduction of the ﬂow of immigration.
The consumer price index (CPI) recorded an
annual growth rate of 3.0% in the fourth
quarter of 2017 and 2.7% for the year. Major
contributors to the CPI growth were the rise in
prices for clothing, food and fuel. In November
2017, the Bank of England conducted its ﬁrst
increase, since Brexit, of its reﬁnancing rate,
by 25 basis points to 0.5% (Chart 1.6). The
Bank of England kept its inﬂation target at
2.0%. At the same time, the Bank of England
decided to maintain the stock of corporate
14


bond purchases at 10 billion pounds and the
stock of government bond purchases – at 435
billion pounds. These quantitative adjustments
are part of a program aimed to stimulate
economic activity and represent the total
volume of acquisitions carried out by the Bank
of England.

China’s economy grew 6.8% in 2017,
its
growth rate increased by 0.1 p.p. compared to
2016,
thus
exceeding
the
Chinese
government’s anticipated growth rate of 6.5%.
Economic performance has been fuelled by
exports against the background of rising global
demand, in particular for electronic equipment.
At
the
same
time,
the
growth
rate
of
investments in ﬁxed assets dropped to 7.2% in
2017 compared to 8.1% in 2016. The growth
of other important sectors, such as retail sales,
declined
to
10.2%,
whereas
industrial
production recorded an increase of 6.6%. By
the end of 2017, annual inﬂation in China
recorded 1.8%, a slower growth of consumer
price index was mainly attributed to the fall in
food prices.

1.3
Evolution
of
the
neighbouring
countries’
economies
and
the
main
trading
partners
Romania
In 2017, Romanian economy grew by 7.0%,
having recorded one of the highest economic
growth in the European Union. This signiﬁcant
increase was driven by the growth of industrial
production by 8.2%, on average.
Annual
inﬂation rate reverted to the target set by the
National Bank of Romania in the second half of
2017. The supply factors had a predominant
impact on consumer prices. Thus, in December
2017, consumer prices rose by 3.3% compared
to
December
2016,
the
annual
average
inﬂation rate having recorded 1.3% (Table 1.2).
Russian Federation
In 2017, the Russian Federation economy
grew,
on
average,
by
1.5%
after
two
consecutive years of economic decline. This
output was achieved due to rising oil prices
and the absorption of shocks generated by
external economic sanctions. The delay in the
implementation of internal structural reforms
has held back its economic potential.
Thus,
during 2017, industrial production increased
by an average of 1.0%, retail trade – by 1.2%,
and exports and imports – by 25.8% and
24.1%,
respectively.
In
the
context
of
excessive deceleration of the annual inﬂation
rate in 2017, the Central Bank of Russia
decreased its base rate six times from 10.0%
to 7.75% (Table 1.2).
Ukraine
Although in 2017 Ukraine’s gross domestic
product increased by an average of 2.2%, the
economic outlook remains challenging as the
country’s economic growth is too slow to
return to the pre-crisis level of 2015. In 2017,
industrial
production
increased
by
0.4%,
agricultural production decreased by 2.7% and
construction activity grew by 20.9%. At the
same time, exports and imports increased by
19.0% and 26.4%, respectively.
In 2017,
consumer prices rose signiﬁcantly, the annual
average inﬂation rate recording 14.4% (Table
1.2).
15


Chapter 2
Economy of the Republic of Moldova
2.1
Real sector
Gross domestic product
In 2017, GDP grew by 4.5%, registering a
similar growth rate to that of 2016. In the ﬁrst
half of 2017, the economy of the Republic of
Moldova recorded a lower annual growth of
2.8%.
Economic growth accelerated in the
second half of the year, with GDP growing in
the third and fourth quarters by 5.4% and
6.4%,
respectively.
Favourable
weather
conditions, recorded in 2017, contributed to an
increase in agricultural output and a high
productivity of the agricultural sector.

On the demand side (Chart 2.1), a pickup in
economic
activity
in
2017
was
largely
determined by an increase in exports of 12.7%,
which contributed 5.5 p.p. to the GDP growth.
This contribution was mainly attributed to a
higher output of the agricultural sector in the
second half of the year. At the same time, a
signiﬁcant contribution (4.2 p.p.)
to the
economic growth in 2017 was generated by an
increase in ﬁnal household consumption of
4.9%, compared to 2016. In the ﬁrst half of the
year,
its
evolution
was
rather
moderate,
reﬂecting
a
modest
domestic
demand.
Subsequently, in the second half of the year,
consumption growth was generated by the
agricultural
sector,
as
evidenced
of
an
increase in the "consumption of goods and
services in kind" component. At the end of the
year, there was an acceleration in the growth
rate of the "procurement of consumer goods"
component.
Government ﬁnal consumption
decreased
by
0.1%,
compared
to
2016.
However, in 2017, gross ﬁxed capital formation
increased by 5.3%, compared to 2016. At the
same time, the "inventory" component has
generated a positive contribution of 1.7 p.p. to
GDP growth.
The evolution of economic
activity in 2017 was signiﬁcantly diminished by
the increase in imports by 11.4%, compared to
2016.
The increase in imports was caused
both by an increase in domestic demand and
appreciation of domestic currency.

If
we
examine
the
major
factors
that
contributed to GDP growth, we conclude that
the agricultural sector and net product taxes
had the largest positive contribution (Chart
2.2).
Thus,
the gross value added from
agriculture increased by 7.9%, compared to
2016.
At the same time, net product taxes
grew by 8.6% in the reporting year, generating
a contribution of 1.3 p.p.
to GDP growth.
Positive contributions were generated by an
increase in gross value added from trade,
industry and transport and storage by 7.0%,
3.0% and 6.2%, respectively. At the same time,
gross value added from construction and the
information
and
communications
sectors
increased by 4.3% and 3.0%, respectively,
compared to 2016.
16


Household consumption
In 2017, ﬁnal household consumption rose by
4.9%, recording a growth rate of 1.3 p.p.
higher than in 2016.
These dynamics were
mainly determined by the increase in the
consumption of consumer goods, which rose
by 4.9%, generating a contribution of 3.4 p.p.
to the annual growth rate of ﬁnal household
consumption (Chart 2.3). At the same time,
the annual growth rate of natural consumption
amounted to 6.8%, resulting in a contribution
of 1.1 p.p., being supported by the positive
evolution of the agricultural sector.
In the
reference period, expenditure on services rose
by 1.7%, generating a contribution of 0.4 p.p.
to the annual growth rate of ﬁnal household
consumption.

The
positive
evolution
of
household

9.0% or 3.7 p.p. above the 2016 level (Chart
2.4). These dynamics were largely determined
by income obtained from salaries and social
beneﬁts, which increased by 11.7% and 15.3%,
respectively, compared to 2016. At the same
time, income obtained from non-agricultural
individual activity decreased by 9.7% during
the reference period. The annual growth rate
of the disposable income of the population, in
real terms, represented 2.2%, being 3.2 p.p.
higher than in 2016.

of

combined
with
the
public
administration
efforts,
contributed
to
the
increase
in
investments in 2017.

Agricultural production
After an increase in agricultural production,
recorded
in
2016,
the
output
of
global
agricultural
production,
recorded
in
the
reporting year, increased by 8.6%, compared
to 2016 (Graﬁcul 2.7).
This evolution was mainly determined by the
increase in the vegetable production output by
13.1%.
It should be noted that 2017 was a
favourable
year
for
all
agricultural
crops,
except for potatoes, which recorded a 3.8%
decrease in the average yield per hectare. At
the same time, in 2017, compared to 2016, a
change in farmers’ preferences for higher
yielding crops (rape, sugar beet, sunﬂower,
corn, vegetables) was noticed, thus replacing
the lower yielding crop such as wheat.
The reduction of production output in the
livestock sector (-2.1%) was determined by the
negative evolution of milk production (-3.6%)
as well as cattle and poultry (-2.8%). At the
same time, the increase in the production of
eggs by 5.0% has offset some of the negative
effects generated by the decrease in milk and
meat production.

was mainly attributed to the growth of the

in

industrial
production

was

in

as

and

industry,
the

of

and

products
(16.4%),
manufacturing of motor vehicles, trailers and
semi-trailers (12.0%) and processing of wood,
manufacturing of wood and cork products,
except furniture; manufacturing of articles of
straw
and
other
plant
weaving
materials
(11.4%).
18


Freight shipping
In 2017, rail, road, river and air transport
companies
carried
23.8%
more
freight,
compared to 2016 (Chart 2.9).
In 2017, out of the total volume of freight
shipped, 71.4% was shipped by road, 27.9%
by rail, 0.8% by river, and 0.01% by air.
A
signiﬁcant increase in freight shipping was
determined by the increase in the volume of
freight carried by rail (37.9%), by road (19.3%)
and by air (2.2-fold), in annual terms. At the
same
time,
the
amount
of
ﬂuvial
cargo
shipped declined, in annual terms, by 0.6%.

Retail trade
In 2017, the total turnover generated by retail
sales businesses decreased by 4.6%, compared
to 2016. At the same time, the total turnover
generated by businesses providing services
as their main activity recorded a decrease of
0.4%, in annual terms (Chart 2.10).
Retail
sales did not include sales of motor vehicles,
motorcycles and their spare parts, sales of
agricultural products by individuals, sales of
grains, seeds, metals, crude oil, chemicals for
industry, iron and steel, industrial machinery
and equipment, sales of food and beverages
for consumption on the premises and sales
of packaged foods, renting of personal and
household goods to the general public.
Wholesale trade
In 2017, the turnover generated by wholesale
businesses as their main activity increased by
19.9% (in current prices), as compared to 2016
(Chart 2.11). At the same time, the turnover
of B2B enterprises providing services recorded
an increase of 8.8% (in current prices) over the
same period, compared to 2016. Wholesale
trade included activities of resale of goods in
large quantities. Sales intermediation activities,
both on domestic and external markets, were
also attributed to the wholesale trade.

Foreign trade
The year 2017 marked a notable revival of
foreign trade3 of the Republic of Moldova4. A
rich agricultural harvest,
which has been
recorded over the last two years, a revival of
the
external
demand
as
a
result
of
the
acceleration of economic activity in the main
partner countries, which was associated with
the increase in the disposable income of the
population (increase in wages, remittances,
social beneﬁts), can be considered the main
factors that have determined the dynamics of
foreign trade. As a result, in 2017 compared to
2016,
exports,
expressed
in
U.S.
dollars,
increased by 18.6%, while imports by 20.2%.

Exports
Exports by countries (Chart 2.12) registered a
positive
evolution
during
2017,
mainly
attributable to an increased demand from EU
countries. Exports to countries classiﬁed as
"the rest of the world" generated a positive
contribution
to
the
annual
growth
rate,
however, it was well below export ﬁgures to
EU
countries.
Exports
to
the
Russian
Federation (RF) increased by 9.2%, however,
as a share of total exports, they declined to a
historical low of 10.5%.
Statistical data
regarding exports to the CIS countries do not
provide clear evidence of a revival of trade
with the countries in the region.

By category of goods (Chart 2.13), a notable
acceleration of the annual export rate in 2017
was mainly determined by the increase in
exports of agricultural products. Thus, record
crops in agriculture, recorded in 2016 and
2017, coupled with an increased EU demand
for these products, created the prerequisites
for a steady export growth in 2017. Exports to
the
EU
countries
increased
by
19.9%,
compared to 2016.
At the same time, an increase in exports of
vehicles, optical devices and sound recording
and transmission devices, driven by positive
developments that took place in the respective
industrial sectors, especially free economic
zones, contributed to the positive dynamics of
the annual export rate.
It should be mentioned that a notable increase
in exports during 2017 was driven by exports
of local goods. Re-exports have also positively
contributed to the evolution of exports, yet had
a lower net impact.
20


Imports
During 2017, imports volume rose with most
trading partners. The major contribution (about
50%) to the evolution of Moldovan imports was
generated by the EU. Imports from the CIS
countries and the rest of the world recorded
lower contributions (Chart 2.15).

In 2017, the unemployment rate registered a
level of 4.1%, being slightly lower (by 0.1 p.p.)
than that recorded in 2016. At the same time,
the
activity
rate
of
economically
active
population recorded 42.2%, being 0.4 p.p.
lower than in 2016, whereas the employment
rate dropped to 40.5%, having decreased 0.4
p.p., compared to 2016 (Chart 2.18).
Personal income
In 2017, the payroll fund in the economy
increased
by
12.9%,
compared
to
2016,
whereas, deﬂated by CPI, it rose by 5.9%. At
the
same
time,
the
average
number
of
employees
in
the
national
economy
has
reduced by 4.9%5 (Chart 2.19).
The average salary in the national economy
increased by 12.1%, compared to 2016.
In
the public and real sectors of the economy the
5NBM ﬁgures based on the quarterly preliminary data.
average salary increased by 13.5% and 11.3%,
respectively. In the reporting year, the annual
growth rate of average salary recorded 5.2%,
in real terms (Chart 2.20). The average real
salary grew by 4.4% in the real sector and by
6.5% in the public sector.
22


2.2
Evolution of inﬂation
Consumer Price Index (CPI)
In 2017, the annual average CPI rate amounted
to 6.6%, recording a level 0.2 p.p. higher than
in 2016. In the ﬁrst half of 2017, the annual
inﬂation rate recorded a prominent upward
trajectory, increasing from 2.4% in December
2016 to 7.4% in May 2017, thus having crossed
the interval of 5.0% ± 1.5 p.p. stipulated in the
medium-term monetary policy strategy.

rate, recorded in the ﬁrst half of the year, and
its retention above the inﬂation target interval
in the second half of the year was mainly
determined by an increase in tariffs for a
number of regulated services and atypical
weather conditions that triggered an increase
in prices for fruits and vegetables, thus having
generated inﬂationary food price pressures
(Chart 2.23).

At the same time, the respective inﬂation
trajectory was supported by the impact of the
adjustment of excise duties on some product
categories as well as by the effect of a low
base period in 2016. The inﬂationary pressures
associated with the aforementioned factors
were partially mitigated by the appreciation of
the domestic currency in 2017, but also by a
persistent low aggregate demand. As a result,
in 2017, the annual rate of core inﬂation
recorded a relatively stable evolution, largely
oscillating around the inﬂation target. In the
context of the rising international oil prices,
fuel prices have had a positive, yet minor,
impact on the annual CPI rate in 2017.
Core inﬂation
During 2017, core inﬂation recorded values
signiﬁcantly below those of total inﬂation,
remaining close to the inﬂation target.
It is
worth noting that this inﬂation excludes the
transient effects on the CPI and largely reﬂects
the inﬂationary pressures associated with
excess demand.
Core inﬂation continued to
remain within the target range for more than a
year.
However, in 2017, the annual rate of core
inﬂation recorded a slightly upward trajectory,
rising from 4.5% in December 2016 to 5.5% in
December 2017.
23


Given a persistent low domestic demand, the
above trend has been supported by a series of
sectoral shocks. Thus, core inﬂation pressures
were building over 2017, being generated, in
the ﬁrst part of the year, by a number of ﬁscal
policy decisions adopted for 20176 and were
further supported by the adoption of additional
regulations on the minimum sales price for
cigarettes7, which resulted in a sharp increase
in their prices.
In 2017, the annual core inﬂation was mainly
fuelled
by
rising
prices
for
such
subcomponents
as
cigarettes,
clothing,
footwear,
public
catering,
education
and
training, the latter as a result of the rise in fees
for nurseries and kindergartens that could be
attributed to the measures taken in order to
increase
the
level
of
funds
allocated
for
children’s nutrition in educational institutions8
(Chart 2.24).

At the same time, the inﬂationary pressures
were partly mitigated by the fall in prices for

by the appreciation of the domestic currency.
Thus, in 2017 compared to 2016, the average
ofﬁcial exchange rate of the Moldovan leu
recorded an appreciation of 7.2% against the
US dollar and 5.6% against the European
single currency.
At the end of 2017, the annual dynamics of
core inﬂation was determined, to a large extent,

by the increase in cigarette prices (26.8%),
education and training (9.2%), clothing (ready-
to-wear clothes - by 6.2%, knitwear - by 4.5%),
footwear (6.3 %).
Food prices
During 2017, the annual rate of food prices
recorded a signiﬁcant upward dynamic, having
risen from 2.8% in December 2016 to 9.7%.
The upward dynamics of the annual rate was
mainly
driven
by
higher
contributions
generated by subcomponents, such as fresh
vegetables, potatoes, fresh fruits and meat,
meat products and canned meat (Chart 2.25).

The evolution of food prices in 2017 was
affected by the snowfalls on 20-21 April and by
the
August
drought,
thus
creating
unfavourable weather conditions for growing
fruits and vegetables.
Besides, inﬂationary
pressures have built as a result of some
sectoral shocks that have affected the prices
for meat and a number of imported products.
The rise in fresh meat prices has been driven
by price increases implemented in some of the
importing countries from the region and by the
bans imposed on the import of meat from
Ukraine (due to outbreaks of swine fever). The
sale price increases that were recorded for
some categories of fresh vegetables and fruits
could be attributed to the increase in their
import prices. Besides, the impact of atypical
weather conditions, recorded in the spring of
2017, was felt in the second half of the year
through a steady increase in prices for fresh
fruits.
At the same time, the impact of the
aforementioned
inﬂationary
factors
was
mitigated by the appreciation of the exchange
rate, as well as by good harvests recorded for
other crops.
24


At the end of the year, in December 2017, the
annual dynamics of food prices was largely
driven by rising prices for vegetables (39.4%),
meat and meat products (8.1%), fresh fruits
(22.6%), potatoes (27.6%).
Regulated prices
In the ﬁrst nine months of 2017, the annual
growth rate of regulated prices recorded a
strong upward trend, rising from (-1.1%) in
December 2016 to 7.5% in September 2017.
Subsequently, it reversed its trend, slightly
decreasing
to
6.1%
in
December
2017
(Chart2.26).

medicines prices as well as a lack of further

services. Such medicines price developments

can be partly attributed to the appreciation of

Fuel prices
In 2017, the annual growth rate of fuel prices
was characterised by an increased volatility.
Thus, in the ﬁrst two months of the year, the
annual growth rate of fuel prices recorded a
strong upward trajectory, increasing from 1.6%
in December 2016 to 12.9% in February 2017,
being mainly driven by oil price developments
recorded during that period.

In this way, in the ﬁrst quarter of 2017, the
average price of Urals oil recorded values
63.2% higher than those at the beginning of
2016,
thus
triggering
a
considerable
intensiﬁcation of pressures generated by fuel
prices on the domestic market (Chart 2.27).
At the same time, a smaller contribution was
generated by the adjustment of excise duties
on
petroleum
products
in
early
2017.
Subsequently, given a gradual easing of oil
price pressures on the international markets
and a steady trend towards appreciation of the
domestic currency against the US dollar, the
annual growth rate of fuel prices recorded a
downward trajectory on the local market,
falling to 5.9% in July 2017.
In August, the
annual growth rate of fuel prices brieﬂy soared
25


up to 9.8%, having reverted to its downward
trend shortly after that, recording a rate of
5.1% at end of 2017. This evolution was driven
by the appreciation of the domestic currency
against the US dollar, which has mitigated the
inﬂationary
pressures
associated
with
oil
prices on the international markets, but also
by the easing of pressures generated by the
propane gas component.
During 2017, a
positive impact on the annual growth rate of
fuel prices was also exerted by the evolution of
prices for ﬁrewood component.
Coal prices
generated
a
minor
contribution
to
the
evolution of fuel prices in 2017.
Industrial prices
In the ﬁrst half of 2017, the annual industrial
production price index (IPPI) recorded a slightly
upward trajectory. Since September, however,
its trajectory resumed a downward trend. In
December, the annual IPPI recorded 2.3%,
being 1.7 p.p. lower than in August 2017 and
0.5 p.p. lower than in December 2016.
Thus, in the fourth quarter of 2017, it recorded
a growth rate of 2.9% or 1.0 p.p. lower than in
the third quarter of 2017. A downward trend of
the annual IPPI was driven by the evolution of
prices for products traded on both the internal
and external markets (Chart 2.28).

2.3
Public
sector
and
tax
policy
State budget execution
In 2017, state budget indicators conﬁrmed the
optimization of tax administration. Following
the increase in public revenues, on the one
hand,
and
a
slower
increase
of
public
expenditures, on the other hand, the budget
deﬁcit diminished and recorded a value of 1
144.7 million lei. According to the Ministry of
Finance, the revenue inﬂows to the state
budget amounted to 53 379.4 million lei,
recording an increase of 16.2%, compared to
2016 (Chart 2.32). In terms of GDP, in 2017,
the accrued revenues increased by 1.6 p.p.,
compared to 2016, accounting for 35.5% of
GDP.

process
required
to
modernize
and
relaunch the economy of the Republic of
Moldova.
In
the
reference
year,
the
state
budget
expenditures totalled 54 524.1 million lei,
recording an increase of 12.5%, compared to
2016 (Chart 2.32). Of the total expenditures,
the
highest
share
was
spent
on
social
protection (35.0%), 17.8% being allocated for
education and 13.3% – for health care.
In
terms of GDP, public expenditures accounted
for 36.3% of GDP, having increased by 0.5 p.p.,
compared to 2016.
It should be mentioned that 18 086.5 million
lei
(up
14.5%,
compared
to
2016)
were
transferred,
during
2017,
from
the
state
budget to the State Social Insurance Budget
(SSIB), the Mandatory Health Insurance Fund
(MHIF) and to the local budgets (LBs).
The
largest
share
of
budget
allocations
was
channelled to the local budgets, around 52.8%,
followed by the allocations made to the SSIB,
around 32.8%,
the MHIF having received
smaller ﬁnancing accounting for 14.3% of total
allocations.
In this context, in 2017, the execution of the
state budget resulted in a deﬁcit of 1 144.7
million lei.
In terms of GDP, the deﬁcit
accounted for 0.8% of GDP, having decreased
by 1.0 p.p. compared to the level of 2016. The
sources
of
ﬁnancing
of
the
state
deﬁcit
included net external loans amounting to 2
349.3 million lei, accounting for 1.6% of GDP
(down 1.0 p.p., compared to 2016), internal
debts and loans amounting to 1 071.3 million
lei, and a negative contribution of ﬁnancial
assets amounting to 271.4 million lei, minus
0.2% of GDP (down 0.4 p.p., compared to
2016). At the same time, the state account
balances increased by 2 004.5 million lei,
compared to the beginning of the year.
State debt
At the end of 2017, the state debt of the
Republic of Moldova amounted to 51 660.3
million lei, accounting for 34.4% of GDP. The
state debt increased by 874.5 million lei or by
1.7% due to the increase of the internal state
debt balance by 1 058.9 million lei, whereas
the
balance
of
the
external
state
debt
decreased by 184.4 million lei. As a share of
GDP, state debt diminished as a result of a
28


stronger evolution of the national economy
(Chart 2.33).
In 2017, the share of internal
state debt in GDP decreased by 0.9 p.p.,
whereas
the
share
the
external
debt,
recalculated in lei, decreased by 2.3 p.p.
As of 31 December 2017, the state debt
constituted 56.3% of external debt and 43.7%
of internal debt.
The balance of the external debt, expressed in
foreign currency, as of 31 December 2017,
increased by 16.1%, compared to January 1,
2017, and amounted to 1 700.7 million U.S.
dollars. The external state debt, expressed in
MDL, decreased during the reporting year by
0.6%, largely due to the appreciation of the
domestic
currency,
totalling
29 081.8 million lei.

At the end of 2017, the state debt recorded
22 578.5 million lei, having increased by 4.9%,
compared to the end of 2016. The increase in
internal state debt by about 18.1% was the
result of additional issues of government
securities on the primary market. In October
2017, MDL 50.0 million worth of one-year
government securities were redeemed. These
securities
covered
payment
obligations
derived from State guarantees. As a result, at
the end of 2017,
the internal state debt
consisted of government securities issued for
the execution of State guarantees (58.9%),
government securities issued on the primary
market (32.0%) and government convertible
securities (9.1%).
In
2017,
1
929.0
million
lei
(up
10.4%,
compared to 2016) were appropriated from
the state budget for the state’s debt service.
The
external
debt
service
appropriations
amounted
to
357.9
million
lei
(having
increased by 25.8%, compared to 2016).
It
should be mentioned that in order to support
the internal state debt service there were
appropriated funds in the amount of 1 571.1
million lei (up 7.4%, compared to 2016), out of
which: 808.2 million lei were appropriated for
the payment of interest and coupons on
government securities issued on the primary
market, 618.2 million lei accounted for interest
paid on government securities issued in 2016
for the execution by the Ministry of Finance of
the payment obligations derived from state
guarantees no. 807 of 17 November 2014 and
no.101 of April 1, 2015, which amounted to
13.3 billion lei, as well as 144.7 million lei were
appropriated for the payment of interest on
government convertible securities.
Public sector debt
As of 31 December 2017, the balance of the
public sector debt decreased by 851.4 million
lei or by 1.4%, compared to the end of 2016
and recorded 58 451.7 million lei.
According to operating statistical data on GDP
in 2017, the share of public sector debt in GDP,
as on 31 December 2017, accounted for 38.9%,
recording a decrease of 4.9 p.p., compared to
the end of 2016.
The decrease of the public sector debt balance
at the end of 2017, compared to the end of
2016, was determined, in particular, by the
decrease of the NBM debt balance by 1 320.5
million lei. Also, the balance of the direct debt
of state enterprises decreased by 386.8 million
lei and the direct debt of administrative
territorial units (ATU) – by 18.6 million lei. At
the
same
time,
the
state
debt
balance
increased by 874.5 million lei, still accounting
for the largest share of the total public sector
debt (88.4%).
As of 31 December 2017, the external debt of
the public sector amounted to 1 998.8 million
U.S. dollars (equivalent to 34 179.2 million lei)
or 58.5% of the public sector debt balance,
whereas the internal debt of the public sector
amounted to 24 272.5 million lei (41.5%).
During
2017,
the
public
sector
debt
diminished, being mainly inﬂuenced by the
external debt of the public sector,
which
recorded a downward evolution of 3.8%.
29

International
accounts
of
the
Republic
of

in
2017
(provisional data)
Balance of payments11
In 2017, the current account balance of the
Republic of Moldova’s balance of payments
recorded a deﬁcit of USD 616.94 million,
having increased 2.2-fold, compared to 2016
(Table A.1). In terms of GDP, it recorded -7.6%
(-3.4 p.p. compared to 2016) (Chart 2.34). The
current account deﬁcit can be attributed to
foreign trade in goods, the negative balance of
which
grew
by
23.0%,
whereas
other
components
recorded
increasing
surplus
balances.

In
2017,
exports
of
goods
and
services
amounted to USD 3 110.43 million, having
increased by 19.9%, compared to 2016, as a
result of the positive impact generated by the
demand
from
the
main
trading
partner
countries (both from the CIS and the EU),
which have recorded economic growth. At the
same time, a negative impact on exports had
the appreciation of the domestic currency
against the U.S. dollar and the euro, which has
affected the exports’ competitiveness on the
foreign market.
Imports of goods and services amounted to
USD 5 364.14 million, an increase of 20.2%,
11A complete analytic presentation of the evolution of

internationale-ale-republicii-moldova-
pentru-anul-2017 -Data-interim-material.
compared to 2016.
Growth in imports was
mainly driven by the increase in ﬁnal household
consumption due to growth in real salaries and
remittances.
The resumption of imports of
electricity from Ukraine also contributed to the
increase in total imports.
In 2017, the volume of foreign trade in
goods (FOB-FOB) amounted to USD 6 284.48
million, up by 21.3%, compared to 2016. The
trade balance deﬁcit totalled USD 2 569.00
million, an increase of 23.0%, compared to
2016, as imports growth (+21.8%) exceeded
exports growth (+20.1%).
The ratio of foreign trade deﬁcit in goods to GDP
recorded 31.6%, having increased by 0.8 p.p.,
compared to 2016. The coverage of imports of
goods and services through exports recorded
58%.
During 2017, the total volume of goods, both
exported and imported, increased compared to
2016 ﬁgures. The prices of the exported goods
decreased
in
the
ﬁrst
half
of
2017
and
increased in the second half of the year,
whereas the prices of the imported goods kept
increasing starting with the second quarter of
2017 through the end of the year.
The
exchange ratio in foreign trade in goods
recorded a slight decrease during the ﬁrst
three quarters of 2017, having eventually
increased in the fourth quarter.
The FOB-based value of exports of goods in
the
balance
of
payments
recorded
USD
1 857.74
million
(+
20.1%
compared
to
2016)12.
In the reporting year, the structure of exports
of goods (excluding processed or otherwise
modiﬁed
goods),
by
geographical
region,
denotes that 58.6% of goods were shipped to
EU countries, 25.4% – to the CIS countries, and
the remaining 16.0% of exports were destined
for other countries (Chart 2.35).
In 2017,
exports to the European Union continued to
grow (+25.3% compared to 2016).
This
increase was mainly driven by the increase in
exports to: Romania – by 21.5%, Germany – by
37.6% and Poland – by 47.0%. Exports to the
CIS have also recorded a rising trend (+11.9%)
as a result of the increase in exports to: Russia
– by 9.3%, Ukraine – by 31.9% and Belarus –
by 6.3%.
The increase in exports to other
countries (+20.7%) was driven by a 2-fold
increase in exports to Turkey (which can be
attributed both to Turkey’s economic growth
12Unlike NBS data, statistics on foreign trade in goods
published by the NBM in the balance of payments contain
a series of adjustments.
30


and the entry into force (as of 01.11.2016) of
the bilateral Free Trade Agreement), to Serbia –
2.9-fold, and China – by 29.7%.
Analysing
exports
by
product
category,
agricultural products continued to hold the
largest share of 59.7%, exports of alcoholic
beverages increased by 17.3%, compared to
2016. In addition, an increase in exports of
machines and appliances was recorded (up by
74.1%, mainly due to a 2.6-fold increase in
exports of cabling and wire), furniture (up by
13.7%), chemicals (up by 8.3%), textiles and
textile articles (rising by 5.3%) and other.
The FOB-based value of imports of goods in
the balance of payments reached USD 4 226.74
million (+21.8% compared to 2016).

followed by the CIS countries – 28.7% and
other countries – 16.3% (Chart 2.36).

imported
goods
were
mineral

accounting for a share of 17.5% (up by 22.9%),
agricultural products (with a 16.1% increase
attributed to the rise in imports of sugar and
cigarettes), machines and appliances (up by
30.6%), chemicals (up by 14.0%), common
metals and metal articles (up by 25.7%),
transport vehicles and equipment
(up by
27.0% as a result of the increase in imports of
cars and tractors).

electricity amounted to USD 746.59 million,
increasing by 23.5% compared to 2016. This

growth was largely driven by the resumption
of electricity imports from Ukraine, a 2.9-fold
increase in imports of coal and of other energy
products: gasoline – by 20.6%; diesel fuel –

The balance of services surplus grew to USD
315.29 million. It was driven by the increase in
exports by 19.7%, compared to 2016, whereas
the imports of services increased by 13.2%.
The ratio of the surplus of services to GDP
amounted to 3.9%, having increased by 0.7
p.p., compared to 2016 (Table A.2).
A signiﬁcant increase was recorded in exports
of travel services (by 28.2%), in particular for
personal
travel
(+27.5%),
transportation
31


services (by 16.9%) (driven by the increase in
the volume of foreign trade in goods), in
particular air transportation (+27.9%). Exports
of raw materials processing services have also
increased considerably (+27.8%), this increase
can be largely attributed to the new direct
investments made in the manufacturing of
wiring and accessories for the automotive
industry, as well as exports of IT services (by
55.9%).
Analysing the structure of foreign trade with
services, one can note that the largest share,
both in terms of receipts and payments made
for the services, recorded in 2017, was held by
transportation services 32.8% and 36.9%,
respectively. The exports and imports of travel
services accounted for 24.9% and 32.6%,
respectively. The services of processing raw
materials accounted for 15.1% of total service
exports in 2017.
In 2017,
the surplus of primary income
increased
by
10.9%,
compared
to
2016,
amounting to USD 510.80 million (Table A.3).
The amount of compensation paid to resident
employees for work performed for non-resident
employers increased by 17.6%, reaching USD
839.56 million. The effect of the increase was,
however, mitigated by the concurrent increase
in outﬂows of investment income (+36.2%), in
particular dividend income of foreign direct
investors, which increased 2.3-fold, totalling
USD 134.58 million. The ratio of the primary
income
to
GDP
recorded
6.3%,
having
decreased by 0.5 p.p., compared to 2016.
The surplus balance of secondary income
remained
virtually
unchanged
(+0.2%),
compared to 2016, amounting to USD 1 125.97
million. Although personal transfers received
from abroad by Moldovan residents increased
by 6.6%, amounting to USD 800.10 million, the
volume of technical assistance and grants,
received by all sectors under international
cooperation programmes, decreased by 15.3%,
compared to 2016, having dropped to USD
183.73 million (Table A.4). In terms of GDP, the
balance of secondary income accounted for
13.8% of GDP, having decreased by 2.8 p.p.,
compared to 2016.
Personal remittances received by residents
of the Republic of Moldova (an indicator
calculated
additionally
to
the
balance
of
payments), which reﬂect the value of net
employee
compensation
(excluding
the
residents’ travel expenses and taxes paid
abroad),
personal
transfers
and
capital
transfers between households, reached USD
1 559.21 million, accounting for 19.2% of GDP
(having decreased as GDP share, compared to
2016,
yet having increased by 13.2% in
absolute value).
The growth of personal
remittances was mainly driven by the increase
in remittances from the EU countries (+24.9%
compared to 2016), as well as from other
countries.

amount: 30% of short-term debt + 15%
of other portfolio commitments + 5% of
M2 + 5% of exports of goods and services
(covered 331.7%) (Chart 2.42).
In the structure of external liabilities, other
investments continue to hold the largest share
(57.4%),
followed
by
direct
investments
accounting for a share of 41.4%.
The total stock of liabilities in the form of direct
investments, accrued at the end of 2017, was
estimated at USD 3 701.74 million (up by
21.9%), the share of equity and shareholdings
(including reinvested earnings) accounting for
50.3%, debt instrument holding a share of
49.7% (Chart 2.43). The composition of debt
instruments was mixed, loans extended by
foreign direct investors amounting to USD 1
34


132.17 million, trade credit commitments to
foreign direct investors having recorded USD
137.06 million, and other debts to foreign
direct investors – USD 569.67 million.

increased
by

to

(Table A.8).

The public and publicly guaranteed external
debt reached USD 1 999.63 million (28.7% of

debt),

increased
by
12.4%,

USD 4 974.07 million (71.3% of total debt), up
by 11.6%.

debt) was accounted for by non-ﬁnancial
corporations, followed by intra-group lending
under the direct investment (including debt for
the import of natural gas) – 26.4%, public
administration – 24.7%, licensed banks – 6.3%,

share

Government debt to the NBM
As of 31 December 2017, the government debt
to
the
NBM
in
the
form
of
government
securities held in the NBM portfolio amounted
to 15 354.6 million lei and consisted of:
Government bonds (GB), issued and placed
with the NBM on 4 October 2016 in order to
execute the obligations of the Ministry of
Finance deriving from state guarantees no.
807 of 17 November 2014 and no.101 of April
1, 2015 – 13 291.2 million lei. Treasury bills
(TB), held in the NBM’s portfolio following the
conversion of loans previously received by the
Ministry of Finance from the National Bank and
their subsequent reissue – 2 063.4 million lei.
Government bonds were issued at the annual
interest (coupon) rate of 1.4% for the maturity
of 2-9 years, and at the rate of 5.3% annually
for the maturity of 10-25 years.
Treasury bills, held in the NBM’s own portfolio
as a result of the reissues performed during
2017, usually had a 91-day maturity and the
interest rate ranging from 5.16%-7.69% per
year. The weighted average interest rate on
TBs, held in the portfolio at the end of 2017,
recorded 5.82% per year against 7.27% per
year recorded in 2016.
Government
deposits
placed
with
the
National Bank of Moldova
In 2017, the Ministry of Finance placed with
the National Bank of Moldova term deposits
in Moldovan lei with maturity ranging from 38
days to 730 days, in a total volume of 554.9
million lei.
Compared to 2016, the volume of deposits
placed with the NBM has recorded a signiﬁcant
growth, having increased more than 9-fold.
It should be mentioned that, in March 2017,
the terms of placement of term deposits taken
from the Ministry of Finance were modiﬁed.
Thus, deposits are now accepted both for the
purpose of managing cash resources held in
the Treasury Single Account as well as for the
purpose of ensuring the liquidity reserve to
reduce the risk of reﬁnancing the government
debt or to provide funds for monetary policy
implementation,
at the weighted average
36


interest rate applicable on securities with
respective maturity, placed at the last auction.
As of 31 December 2017, the balance of term
deposits placed by the Ministry of Finance
amounted
to
508.9
million
lei,
being
exclusively made up of deposits destined for
the formation of the liquidity reserve.
The
average weighted rate on deposits in the
balance recorded 7.56% annually, whereas the
weighted average term to maturity – 613 days.
The NBM’s role in the public sector’s
development
During
2017,
the
NBM
supported
the
Government of the Republic of Moldova in the
implementation of a series of public policies,
meant to promote the welfare of Moldovan
citizens.
In this respect, it is worth noting the NBM’s
contribution to the elaboration of a series of
documents
of
major
importance
for
the
country, including the Roadmap for Improving
Competitiveness, the National Strategy for
Investment
and
Export
Promotion
for
2016-2020, the Strategy for the Development
of the Small and Medium-Sized Enterprises
Sector for 2012-2020, the Consumer Protection
Strategy for 2013-2020, the Strategy for the
Reform of the Regulatory Framework for the
Entrepreneurial Activity for 2013-2020, the
Action Plan on the Implementation of the
National
Strategy
for
Preventing
and
Combating of Money Laundering and Terrorism
Financing for 2013-2017, the Action Plan for
the Implementation of the Strategy for the
Development
of
the
National
Statistical
System for 2016-2020, and others.
At the same time,
the National Bank of
Moldova
constantly
contributes
to
the
fulﬁlment of the commitments made by the
Moldovan
authorities
to
international
development partners,
which refer to the
NBM’s areas of competence. Given the above,
the NBM participates in the achievement of
the objectives set out in the National Action
Plan for the Implementation of Association
Agreement between the Republic of Moldova
and the European Union during 2017-2019, the
fulﬁlment of the commitments related to the
Economic and Financial Policy Memoranda
(MEFP, SMEFP) signed with the IMF, as well as
contributes to the implementation of structural
reforms
under
the
Memorandum
of
Understanding, singed between the Republic
of
Moldova
and
the
European
Union
on
macro-ﬁnancial assistance to the Republic of
Moldova.
37


Chapter 3
Monetary Policy of the National Bank of Moldova
3.1
Monetary
policy
objective
Pursuant to the provisions of the Law No.
548-XIII of 21 July 1995 on the National Bank
of Moldova, the NBM is the central bank of the
Republic of Moldova and an autonomous public
legal entity that establishes and promotes
monetary and foreign exchange policy in the
state. During 2017, the NBM operated in line
with
the
provisions
of
the
Medium-term
monetary policy strategy, approved by the
Council of Administration of the National Bank
of Moldova on 27 December 2012.
According to the above-mentioned strategy, in
order to ensure and maintain price stability,
the NBM set the inﬂation target, measured by
the consumer price index, which is published
monthly by the National Bureau of Statistics
(NBS) at 5.0% level with a tolerance range of ±
1.5 p.p.
By quantifying the macroeconomic situation,
the
trends
and
the
forecasts
of
the
medium-term macroeconomic indicators, the
short and medium-term inﬂation outlook in the
conditions
of
the
risks
and
uncertainties
recorded in 2017, and in order to ease the
disinﬂationary pressures, the National Bank of
Moldova promoted a stimulating monetary
policy compared to 2016. At the same time,
the conduct of monetary policy was affected
by the complexity of the balance of risks, both
internal and external, a gradual intensiﬁcation
of inﬂationary risks being recorded.
Thus,
during 2017,
the National Bank of
Moldova has been gradually adjusting its
monetary policy in order to stimulate the
national economy through the creation of
favourable conditions for lending.
These
measures were aimed at boosting domestic
demand
alongside
ensuring
a
steady
adjustment
of
the
domestic
economic
environment to the volatility and uncertainty
of the external environment. In this context,
the members of the NBM Executive Board
decided to lower the monetary policy interest
rate during 2017 by 2.5 p.p., having decreased
it from 9.0% to 6.5% annually.
These
decisions
aimed
at
diminishing
disinﬂationary
expectations
through
strengthening the prospects of the inﬂation
rate being kept within the range of ± 1.5 p.p.
from the 5.0% target for subsequent periods.
3.2
Monetary
policy
decisions
The intensiﬁcation of the inﬂationary process
at the beginning of 2017 was in line with the
National Bank’s forecasts, based on which the
annual inﬂation rate has had an upward
trajectory, rising above the limits of ± 1.5 p.p.
of the inﬂation target range, up to the third
quarter of 2017. Subsequently, the inﬂation
rate
assumed
a
downward
trend
and
positioned itself within the inﬂation target
interval,
being
driven
by
disinﬂationary
pressures.
The decisions of the NBM’s Executive Board
adopted in January-May 2017 on the intention
to maintain the base rate at 9.0% per annum
took
into
account
the
existing
inﬂation
forecasts,
the
previous
monetary
policy
decisions and the speed of the transmission of
the effects of these decisions in the economy.
In June 2017, the dynamics of macroeconomic
indicators
denoted
the
prerequisites
for
slowing down of economic activity and a
lowering
of
the
medium-term
inﬂationary
pressures
in
accordance
with
the
NBM’s
forecasts. This fact, in combination with the
appreciation, in real terms, of the domestic
currency, determined the members of the
NBM’s Executive Board to reduce the base rate
applied
to
the
main
monetary
policy
operations by 1.0 p.p.,
from 9.0 to 8.0%
annually.
Later, in August-December 2017, the NBM’s
decisions aimed at gradually implementing a
stimulating monetary policy. To this end, the
NBM has lowered the monetary policy interest
38


rate three times, by 0.5 p.p., eventually setting
a level of 6.5% annually.
The
NBM’s
Executive
Board
decided
to
maintain the base rate at the same level of
6.5% through the end of 2017 based on the
assumption
that
the
monetary
policy
measures, implemented by the NBM at the
beginning of the year, still needed time to
spread through different transmission channels
in the national economy, including through the
level of interest rates applied on loans and
deposits extended/taken in domestic currency,
thus further acting on inﬂation evolution.
It should be mentioned that in order to sterilize
and adequately manage the liquidity surplus
accumulated during 2017 and improve the
mechanism of transmission of monetary policy
decisions in view of ensuring an adequate set
of monetary conditions aimed at achieving the
proposed inﬂation target rate set for the
medium-term, the NBM gradually increased
the level of required reserves ratio of the
means
attracted
in
Moldovan
lei
and
non-convertible currency by 5.0 p.p., up to the
level of 40.0%. At the same time, the required
reserve ratio of funds held in freely convertible
currency was decided to maintain at 14.0%
throughout 2017.
3.3
Monetary
policy
achievements
during
2017
Monetary market operations, standing facilities
and
the
required
reserve
mechanism
continued
to
play
a
key
role
in
the
implementation of monetary policy. Monetary
market operations were carried out at the
NBM’s initiative, the applied interest rate being
equal to the monetary policy rate, whereas
standing facilities were used at the bank’s
discretion.
At the same time,
the NBM
strengthened the restrictive character of the
required reserve mechanism by increasing by
5.0 p.p. the reserve requirement set for banks’
liabilities in Moldovan lei, whereas maintaining
the reserve requirement set for banks’ foreign
currency liabilities at the level of the previous
year.
Monetary market operations
The
excess
liquidity,
accumulated
in
the
banking system at the end of 2016, increased
in 2017,
including as a result of foreign
exchange
interventions
(foreign
currency
purchases) amounting to USD 433.8 million
(7.8 billion lei), and determined an increased
sterilization effort on the NBM’s side, the
volume
of
these
operations
increasing
signiﬁcantly, compared to the previous year
(Chart 3.1).

Issuance of NBM Certiﬁcates
The NBM has issued NBC through auctions,
which were carried out twice per week in the
ﬁrst four months of the year and weekly since
May. According to 2017 ﬁgures, the weighted
average
rate
on
NBC
recorded
8.11%,
compared to 11.55% of the previous year.
Reﬂecting a signiﬁcant increase in the banks’
net
positive
liquidity,
the
volume
of
liquidity-absorbing
operations
doubled,
compared to 2016, a condition that was also
reﬂected in the annual average volume of NBC
stock, which rose from 2 959.0 million lei in
2016 to 6 358.1 million lei in 2017.
The
developments in daily NBC balance followed a
regular pattern characterised by its decline at
the end of the reserve maintenance period and
a subsequent return to higher levels at the
beginning of the next period. The daily NBC
balance recorded its minimum value of 3 502.9
million lei at the beginning of May as a result
of the increase in the reserve requirement
ratio set for the placements held in Moldovan
lei and non-convertible currency, as well as
due to a reduced frequency of NBC auctions
starting with May. The maximum amount of
9 207.2 million lei was recorded at the end of
the year as a result of the stronger purchases
of foreign currency conducted by the NBM in
August-October 2017.
39


Lending activity
As of December 31, 2017, the credit exposure
of the licensed banks to the National Bank of
Moldova amounted to 0.1 million lei, being
entirely accounted by loans extended to banks
in 1992-2002 for lending the construction of
housing cooperatives (CHC). Compared to the
end of 2016, the balance of loans extended to
banks decreased by 0.3 million lei.
The loan portfolio’s assessment, carried out in
December 2017, identiﬁed no write-downs for
loan losses.
Standing facilities
The
conditions
of
operation
of
standing
facilities
(overnight
deposits
and
loans),
established by the NBM, have ensured for
banks an increased ﬂexibility and efﬁciency of
liquidity management.
During 2017, interest rates applied on standing
facilities decreased, the overnight deposit rate
dropping from 6.00% annually at the beginning
of the year to 3.50% annually at the end of the
year, whereas the overnight loan rate fell from
12.00% to 9.50% annually.
Overnight deposit facility
Banks have used the overnight deposit facility
mainly at the end of the reserve maintenance
periods. The frequency of using the deposit
facility was generally the same throughout the
maintenance periods of the required reserves
held in Moldovan lei. Thus, the average annual
balance recorded 1 058.0 million lei, having
increased
by
420.1
million
lei
or
65.9%,
compared to 2016. At the beginning of each
maintenance period, the amount of overnight
deposits
placed
with
the
NBM
was
insigniﬁcant; however, it would increase later
once the banks have fulﬁlled the reserve
requirement. The average monthly balance of
overnight deposits placed by banks recorded
over 1 billion lei during January-November,
whereas in December it fell to 693.1 million lei
(Chart 3.2) as a result of the change in the
liquidity management strategy implemented
by one of the banks.
Overnight credit facility
As in 2016, the level of excess liquidity, which is
monitored in the banking system, determined
the banks’ decreased demand for loan facility.
In 2017, the overnight loan facility was used
mainly at the end of the reserve maintenance

periods, in April and December, by a small

However, the total amount of overnight loans
extended by the NBM during 2017 was higher
than in the previous year, amounting to 357

2016.
Required reserves
The required reserve mechanism continued to
ensure the liquidity control and management
in the banking system.
During 2017, against the background of a
persisting excess of liquidity in MDL, recorded
in the banking sector,
and the potential
pro-inﬂationary risks it could generate, the
NBM has increased the required reserve ratio
of
funds
held
in
Moldovan
lei
and
non-convertible currency by 5.0 p.p.
in two
steps, as follows:
• up to 37% of the calculation base for the
required reserve maintenance period of
April 8, 2017-May 7, 2017;
• up to 40% of the calculation base starting
with the required reserve maintenance
period of May 8, 2017-June 7, 2017.
Given the above and as a result of the
increased amount of placements made with
the banks (Chart 3.3), the required reserves
held
in
MDL
between
December
8,
2017-January 7, 2018 amounted to 12 638.6
million lei, having risen by 25.6%, compared to
the end of the previous year (December 8,
2016-January 7, 2017).
40


At the same time, during the last six years,
the NBM has not changed the foreign currency
reserve requirement ratio (US dollars and euro),
maintaining it at the level of 14.0% of the
calculation base.

As of 31 December 2017, the required foreign
exchange reserves held by banks at the NBM
amounted to USD 70.7 million and EUR 118.5
million (Chart 3.4), recording an increase of
15.3% and 12.3%, respectively, compared to
the end of 2016.
Pursuant to article 17(3) of the Law no.
548-XIII of 21 July 1995 on the National Bank
of Moldova, for the share of required reserves
exceeding 5.0% of the liabilities, based on
which the required reserve level is calculated,
the National Bank paid to banks interest
amounting to 534.3 million lei, by 33.0% less
than in 2016. NBM’s expenditures related to
the required reserves decreased as a result of
the
NBM’s
measures
taken
to
relax
the
monetary policy through the base rate tool. In
this context, during 2017, the interest rate
applied on the required reserves held in MDL
(the average rate on overnight deposit facility)
decreased from 6.0% to 3.56% annually.
Intervention
on
the
domestic
foreign
exchange market
In
2017,
the
National
Bank
of
Moldova
intervened on the domestic forex market only
as a currency buyer to consolidate the ofﬁcial
reserve assets, as a signiﬁcant surplus of
foreign
currency
was
recorded
on
the
intrabank market.
During the reporting period, the volume of net
transactions carried out by the National Bank
of Moldova on the interbank forex market in
Moldovan lei totalled, at value date, USD
436.31 million, including currency conversions
in the form of purchases, carried out with the
World Bank institutions, amounting to USD
2.54 million (Chart 3.5).

3.4
Monetary
and
foreign
exchange conditions
Dynamic of monetary indicators
Throughout
2017,
monetary
indicators
maintained a positive trend. Thus, the average
growth rate of broad money supply recorded
8.7%
in
annual
terms.
Other
monetary
aggregates
recorded
a
similar
behaviour
during the reference year.
Money supply
In January-December 2017, the money supply
(M213) increased by 14.1%, recording the
annual growth rate 4.1 p.p. lower than in 2016
(Chart 3.6). Starting with the second quarter of
2017, the M2 monetary aggregate has slowed
down its annual growth, having reverted to an
upward trend in the fourth quarter.
Both
components
of
money
supply
M2
have
recorded
positive
developments.
Thus,
domestic currency deposits and money in
circulation increased, compared to 2016, by
16.4% or by 4 783.4 million lei and by 10.3%
or 1 778.3 million lei, respectively.

The evolution of domestic currency deposits
was characterised by a gradual slowing down of
their growth rate, recording a minimum growth
rate of 9.4% at the end of the third quarter;
in the last quarter, however, their trend has
reverted to an upward one. Throughout the
year, money in circulation has had a uniform
13Money
supply
M2
includes
money
in
circulation
(M0), domestic currency deposits and money market
instruments.
evolution, recording a growth rate of 1.1 p.p.
lower than in 2016.
At the same time, the money supply (M314)
recorded an annual growth of 9.3%, its growth
rate having decreased by 0.9 p.p., compared
to 2016 (Chart 3.7).
